# Europe

This article is about the continent. For other uses, see [Europe (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_\(disambiguation\)).

**Europe** is a [continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent)[^cnote_t] located entirely in the [Northern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere) and mostly in the [Eastern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Hemisphere). It is bordered by the [Arctic Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Ocean) to the north, the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) to the west, the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the south, and [Asia](689.md) to the east. Europe shares the [landmass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmass) of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) with Asia, and of [Afro-Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia) with both [Africa](5334607.md) and Asia.[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-\:1-10] Europe is commonly considered to be [separated from Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundaries_between_the_continents#Asia_and_Europe) by the [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_divide) of the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains), the [Ural River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_\(river\)), the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md), the [Greater Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Caucasus), the [Black Sea](3386.md), and the [Turkish straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_straits).[^cite_note-NatlGeoAtlas-11]

Europe covers approximately 10,186,000 square kilometres (3,933,000 sq mi), or 2% of [Earth's surface](9228.md#Surface) (6.8% of Earth's land area), making it the second-smallest continent (using the [seven-continent model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent#Number)). [Politically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Europe), Europe is divided into about [fifty sovereign states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Europe#UN_member_states_and_UNGA_non-member_observer_state), of which [Russia](25391.md) is the [largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area) and [most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_population), spanning 39% of the continent and comprising 15% of [its population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Europe). Europe had a total population of about 745 million (about 10% of the [world population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population)) in 2021; the [third-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_continents_and_continental_subregions_by_population) after Asia and Africa.[^cite_note-UN_WPP-1][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-2] The [European climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Europe) is affected by warm Atlantic currents, such as the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream), which produce a [temperate climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate), tempering winters and summers, on much of the continent. Further from the sea, seasonal differences are more noticeable producing more [continental climates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate).

The [culture of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe) consists of a range of national and regional cultures, which form the central roots of the wider [Western civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture), and together commonly reference [ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and [ancient Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome), particularly through [their Christian successors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Europe), as crucial and shared roots.[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13] Beginning with the [fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) in 476 CE, [Christian consolidation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization) of Europe in the wake of the [Migration Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_Period) marked the European [post-classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-classical) [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages). The [Italian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Renaissance) spread across many [Western European](33800.md) countries, adapting to local contexts and giving rise to distinct national expressions. The renewed [humanist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_humanism) emphasis on [art](311401.md) and [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_in_the_Renaissance) was among the several factors that contributed to the broader transition to the modern era. Since the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery), led by [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) and [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire), Europe played a predominant role in global affairs with multiple explorations and conquests around the world. Between the 16th and 20th centuries, [European powers colonised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_former_European_colonies) at various times the [Americas](29833.md), almost all of Africa and [Oceania](22621.md), and the majority of Asia.

The [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment), the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution), and the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) shaped the continent culturally, politically, and economically from the end of the 17th century until the first half of the 19th century. The [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), which began in [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) at the end of the 18th century, gave rise to radical economic, cultural, and social change in [Western Europe](33800.md) and eventually the wider world. Both [world wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_war) began and were fought to a great extent in Europe, contributing to a decline in Western European dominance in world affairs by the mid-20th century as the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and the United States took prominence and competed over [geopolitical dominance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geopolitical_dominance) in Europe and globally.[^cite_note-natgeo_534-14] The resulting [Cold War](325329.md) divided Europe along the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain), with [NATO](21133.md) in the [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) and the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) in the [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc). This divide ended with the [Revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989), the [fall of the Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall), and the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), which allowed [European integration](683704.md) to advance significantly.

European integration has been advanced institutionally since 1948 with the founding of the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe), and significantly through the realisation of the [European Union](9317.md) (EU), which represents today the majority of Europe.[^cite_note-europaeu_1945-59-15] The European Union is a [supranational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supranational_union) [political entity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_identity) that lies between a [confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation) and a [federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation) and is based on a system of [European treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_treaties).[^cite_note-16] The EU originated in Western Europe but [has been expanding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_the_European_Union) eastward since the dissolution of the Soviet Union in 1991. A majority of its members have adopted a common currency, the [euro](9472.md), and participate in the [European single market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_single_market) and [a customs union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Customs_Union). A large bloc of countries, the [Schengen Area](16565139.md), have also abolished internal border and immigration controls. [Regular popular elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_to_the_European_Parliament) take place every five years within the EU; they are considered the second-largest democratic elections in the world after [India's](1435365.md). The [EU economy](1598533.md) is the second-largest in the world by nominal [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) and third-largest by [PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PPP-adjusted_GDP).

## Etymology

Further information: [Europa (consort of Zeus)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_\(consort_of_Zeus\))



Reconstruction of an early [world map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_world_maps) made by [Anaximander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaximander) of the 6th century BCE, dividing the known world into three large landmasses, one of which was named Europe

In classical [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology), [Europa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_\(consort_of_Zeus\)) ([Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): Εὐρώπη, *Eurṓpē*) was a [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia) princess. One view is that her name derives from the Ancient Greek elements εὐρύς (*eurús*) 'wide, broad', and ὤψ (*ōps*, [gen.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genitive_case) ὠπός, *ōpós*) 'eye, face, countenance', hence their composite *Eurṓpē* would mean 'wide-gazing' or 'broad of aspect'.[^cite_note-WestWest2007-17][^cite_note-FitzRoy2015-18][^cite_note-Astour1967-19][^cite_note-etymonline-20] *Broad* has been an [epithet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epithet) of Earth herself in the reconstructed [Proto-Indo-European religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-European_mythology) and the poetry devoted to it.[^cite_note-WestWest2007-17] An alternative view is that of [Robert Beekes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_S._P._Beekes), who has argued in favour of a pre-Indo-European origin for the name, explaining that a derivation from *eurus* would yield a different [toponym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponymy) than Europa. Beekes has located toponyms related to that of Europa in the territory of ancient Greece, and localities such as that of [Europos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europus_\(Almopia\)) in [ancient Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\)).[^cite_note-Beekes-21]

There have been attempts to connect *Eurṓpē* to a Semitic term for *west*, this being either [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language) *erebu* meaning 'to go down, set' (said of the sun) or [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_language) *'ereb* 'evening, west',[^cite_note-etymonline-20] which is at the origin of [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) *maghreb* and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) *ma'arav*. [Martin Litchfield West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Litchfield_West) stated that "phonologically, the match between Europa's name and any form of the Semitic word is very poor",[^cite_note-22] while Beekes considers a connection to Semitic languages improbable.[^cite_note-Beekes-21]

Most major world languages use words derived from *Eurṓpē* or *Europa* to refer to the continent. Chinese, for example, uses the word *Ōuzhōu* (歐洲/欧洲), which is an abbreviation of the transliterated name *Ōuluóbā zhōu* (歐羅巴洲) (*zhōu* means "continent"); a similar Chinese-derived term *Ōshū* (欧州) is also sometimes used in Japanese such as in the Japanese name of the European Union, *Ōshū Rengō* (欧州連合), despite the [katakana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katakana) *Yōroppa* (ヨーロッパ) being more commonly used. In some Turkic languages, the originally Persian name *[Frangistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frangistan)* ("land of the [Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks)") is used casually in referring to much of Europe, besides official names such as *Avrupa* or *Evropa*.[^cite_note-davison-23]

The prevalent definition of Europe as a geographical term has been in use since the mid-19th century. Europe is taken to be bounded by large bodies of water to the north, west and south; Europe's limits to the east and north-east are usually taken to be the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains), the [Ural River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_\(river\)), and the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md); to the south-east, the [Caucasus Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains), the [Black Sea](3386.md), and the waterways connecting the Black Sea to the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md).[^cite_note-Encarta-24]



Definitions used for the [boundary between Asia and Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundaries_between_the_continents) in different periods of history. *B* ([Urals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains)) and *F* ([Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains)) form the modern conception.



A medieval [T and O map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T_and_O_map) printed by [Günther Zainer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCnther_Zainer) in 1472, showing the three continents as domains of the sons of [Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah) – Asia to Sem ([Shem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shem)), Europe to Iafeth ([Japheth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japheth)) and Africa to Cham ([Ham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ham_\(son_of_Noah\)))

Islands are generally grouped with the nearest continental landmass, hence [Iceland](14531.md) is considered to be part of Europe, while the nearby island of Greenland is usually assigned to [North America](21139.md), although politically belonging to Denmark. Nevertheless, there are some exceptions based on sociopolitical and cultural differences. Cyprus is closest to [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) (or Asia Minor), but is considered part of Europe politically[^cite_note-25] and it is a member state of the EU. Malta was considered an island of [North-western Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb) for centuries, but now it is considered to be part of Europe as well.[^cite_note-26] "Europe", as used specifically in [British English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English), may also refer to [Continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe) exclusively.[^cite_note-27]

The term "continent" usually implies the [physical geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_geography) of a large land mass completely or almost completely surrounded by water at its borders. Prior to the adoption of the current convention that includes mountain divides, the border between Europe and Asia had been redefined several times since its first conception in [classical antiquity](252905.md), but always as a series of rivers, seas and straits that were believed to extend an unknown distance east and north from the Mediterranean Sea without the inclusion of any mountain ranges. Cartographer [Herman Moll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Moll) suggested in 1715 that Europe was bounded by a series of partly joined waterways directed towards the Turkish straits, and the [Irtysh River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irtysh_River) draining into the upper part of the [Ob River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ob_River) and the [Arctic Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Ocean). In contrast, the present eastern boundary of Europe partially adheres to the Ural and Caucasus Mountains, which is somewhat arbitrary and inconsistent compared to any clear-cut definition of the term "continent".

The current division of Eurasia into two continents now reflects [East-West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_dichotomy) cultural, linguistic and ethnic differences which vary on a spectrum rather than with a sharp dividing line. The geographic border between Europe and Asia does not follow any state boundaries and now only follows a few bodies of water. Turkey is generally considered a [transcontinental country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_transcontinental_countries) divided entirely by water, while Russia and [Kazakhstan](16642.md) are only partly divided by waterways. France, the Netherlands, Portugal and Spain are also transcontinental (or more properly, intercontinental, when oceans or large seas are involved) in that their main land areas are in Europe while pockets of their territories are located on other [continents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continents) separated from Europe by large bodies of water. Spain, for example, has territories ([Ceuta](6443.md) and [Melilla](20226.md)) south of the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) which are parts of [Africa](5334607.md) and share a border with Morocco. According to the current convention, Georgia and Azerbaijan are transcontinental countries where waterways have been completely replaced by mountains as the divide between continents.

### History of the concept

See also: [Boundary between Europe and Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundary_between_Europe_and_Asia)

#### Early history



Depiction of *[Europa regina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_regina)* ('Queen Europe') in 1582

The first recorded usage of *Eurṓpē* as a geographic term is in the [Homeric Hymn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeric_Hymn) to [Delian Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delian_Apollo), in reference to the western shore of the [Aegean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegean_Sea). As a name for a part of the known world, it is first used in the 6th century BCE by [Anaximander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaximander) and [Hecataeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hecataeus_of_Miletus). Anaximander placed the boundary between Asia and Europe along the Phasis River (the modern [Rioni River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rioni_River) on the territory of [Georgia](48768.md)) in the Caucasus, a convention still followed by [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) in the 5th century BCE.[^cite_note-28] Herodotus mentioned that the world had been divided by unknown persons into three parts—Europe, Asia, and Libya (Africa)—with the [Nile](21244.md) and the Phasis forming their boundaries—though he also states that some considered the [River Don](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_River_\(Russia\)), rather than the Phasis, as the boundary between Europe and Asia.[^cite_note-29] Europe's eastern frontier was defined in the 1st century by geographer [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo) at the River Don.[^cite_note-30] The *[Book of Jubilees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jubilees)* described the continents as the lands given by [Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah) to his three sons; Europe was defined as stretching from the [Pillars of Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pillars_of_Hercules) at the [Strait of Gibraltar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Gibraltar), separating it from [Northwest Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Africa), to the Don, separating it from Asia.[^cite_note-31]

The convention received by the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) and surviving into modern usage is that of the [Roman era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_era) used by Roman-era authors such as [Posidonius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posidonius),[^cite_note-32] [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo),[^cite_note-33] and [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy),[^cite_note-34] who took the Tanais (the modern Don River) as the boundary.

The Roman Empire did not attach a strong identity to the concept of continental divisions. However, following the fall of the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire), the [culture that developed in its place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture), linked to Latin and the Catholic church, began to associate itself with the concept of "Europe".[^cite_note-Pocock2002-35] The term "Europe" is first used for a cultural sphere in the [Carolingian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance) of the 9th century. From that time, the term designated the sphere of influence of the [Western Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Church), as opposed to both the [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) churches and to the [Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world).

A cultural definition of Europe as the lands of [Latin Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) coalesced in the 8th century, signifying the new cultural condominium created through the confluence of Germanic traditions and Christian-Latin culture, defined partly in contrast with [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium) and [Islam](6037917.md), and limited to northern [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberia), the British Isles, France, Christianised western Germany, the Alpine regions and northern and central Italy.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] The concept is one of the lasting legacies of the [Carolingian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance): *Europa* often figures in the letters of Charlemagne's court scholar, [Alcuin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcuin).[^cite_note-38] The transition of Europe to being a cultural term as well as a geographic one led to the borders of Europe being affected by cultural considerations in the East, especially relating to areas under Byzantine, Ottoman, and Russian influence. Such questions were affected by the positive connotations associated with the term Europe by its users. Such cultural considerations were not applied to the Americas, despite their conquest and settlement by European states. Instead, the concept of "Western civilisation" emerged as a way of grouping together Europe and these colonies.[^cite_note-39]

#### Modern definitions

Further information: [Regions of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Europe) and [Continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe)



*A New Map of Europe According to the Newest Observations* (1721) by Hermann Moll draws the eastern boundary of Europe along the Don River flowing south-west and the Tobol, Irtysh and Ob rivers flowing north.



1916 political map of Europe showing most of Moll's waterways replaced by von Strahlenberg's Ural Mountains and Freshfield's Caucasus crest, land features of a type that normally defines a subcontinent

The question of defining a precise eastern boundary of Europe arises in the Early Modern period, as the eastern extension of [Muscovy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) began to include [North Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Asia). Throughout the Middle Ages and into the 18th century, the traditional division of the landmass of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) into two continents, Europe and Asia, followed Ptolemy, with the boundary following the [Turkish Straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Straits), the [Black Sea](3386.md), the [Kerch Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerch_Strait), the [Sea of Azov](78019.md) and the [Don](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_River_\(Russia\)) (ancient [Tanais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanais)). But maps produced during the 16th to 18th centuries tended to differ in how to continue the boundary beyond the Don bend at [Kalach-na-Donu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalach-na-Donu) (where it is closest to the Volga, now joined with it by the [Volga–Don Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga%E2%80%93Don_Canal)), into territory not described in any detail by the ancient geographers.

Around 1715, [Herman Moll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Moll) produced a map showing the northern part of the [Ob River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ob_River) and the [Irtysh River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irtysh_River), a major tributary of the Ob, as components of a series of partly joined waterways taking the boundary between Europe and Asia from the Turkish Straits, and the Don River all the way to the Arctic Ocean. In 1721, he produced a more up to date map that was easier to read. However, his proposal to adhere to major rivers as the line of demarcation was never taken up by other geographers who were beginning to move away from the idea of water boundaries as the only legitimate divides between Europe and Asia.

Four years later, in 1725, [Philip Johan von Strahlenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Johan_von_Strahlenberg) was the first to depart from the classical Don boundary. He drew a new line along the [Volga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_River), following the Volga north until the [Samara Bend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samara_Bend), along [Obshchy Syrt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obshchy_Syrt) (the [drainage divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_divide) between the Volga and [Ural Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_River)), then north and east along the latter waterway to its source in the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains). At this point he proposed that mountain ranges could be included as boundaries between continents as alternatives to nearby waterways. Accordingly, he drew the new boundary north along the Ural Mountains rather than the nearby and parallel running Ob and Irtysh rivers.[^cite_note-40] This was endorsed by the Russian Empire and introduced the convention that would eventually become commonly accepted. However, this did not come without criticism. [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire), writing in 1760 about [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great)'s efforts to make Russia more European, ignored the whole boundary question with his claim that neither Russia, Scandinavia, northern Germany, nor Poland were fully part of Europe.[^cite_note-Pocock2002-35] Since then, many modern analytical geographers like [Halford Mackinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halford_Mackinder) have declared that they see little validity in the Ural Mountains as a boundary between continents.[^cite_note-41]

The mapmakers continued to differ on the boundary between the lower Don and Samara well into the 19th century. The [1745 atlas](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category\:Atlas%20of%20Russian%20Empire.%201745%20year) published by the [Russian Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Academy_of_Sciences) has the boundary follow the Don beyond Kalach as far as [Serafimovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serafimovich_\(town\)) before cutting north towards [Arkhangelsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkhangelsk), while other 18th- to 19th-century mapmakers such as [John Cary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Cary) followed Strahlenberg's prescription. To the south, the [Kuma–Manych Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuma%E2%80%93Manych_Depression) was identified c. 1773 by a German naturalist, [Peter Simon Pallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Simon_Pallas), as a valley that once connected the Black Sea and the Caspian Sea,[^cite_note-oren-icn.ru-42][^cite_note-43] and subsequently was proposed as a natural boundary between continents.

By the mid-19th century, there were three main conventions, one following the Don, the [Volga–Don Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga%E2%80%93Don_Canal) and the Volga, the other following the Kuma–Manych Depression to the Caspian and then the Ural River, and the third abandoning the Don altogether, following the [Greater Caucasus watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Caucasus_watershed) to the Caspian. The question was still treated as a "controversy" in geographical literature of the 1860s, with [Douglas Freshfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Freshfield) advocating the Caucasus crest boundary as the "best possible", citing support from various "modern geographers".[^cite_note-44]

In Russia and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), the boundary along the Kuma–Manych Depression was the most commonly used as early as 1906.[^cite_note-45] In 1958, the Soviet Geographical Society formally recommended that the boundary between Europe and Asia be drawn in textbooks from [Baydaratskaya Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baydaratskaya_Bay), on the [Kara Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Sea), along the eastern foot of Ural Mountains, then following the [Ural River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_River) until the [Mugodzhar Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mugodzhar_Hills), and then the [Emba River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emba_River); and Kuma–Manych Depression,[^cite_note-46] thus placing the Caucasus entirely in Asia and the Urals entirely in Europe.[^cite_note-47] The *[Flora Europaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_Europaea)* adopted a boundary along the [Terek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terek_\(river\)) and [Kuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuban_\(river\)) rivers, so southwards from the Kuma and the Manych, but still with the Caucasus entirely in Asia.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49] However, most geographers in the Soviet Union favoured the boundary along the Caucasus crest,[^cite_note-50] and this became the common convention in the later 20th century, although the Kuma–Manych boundary remained in use in some 20th-century maps.

[Martin W. Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_W._Lewis) and [Kären Wigen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%A4ren_Wigen) argue that the separation of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) into Asia and Europe of vastly uneven sizes produces a false impression of parity between European and Asian countries, as a residue of [Eurocentrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocentrism): "In physical, cultural and historical diversity, China and India are comparable to the entire European landmass, not to a single European country."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewisWigen19979,10-51]

## History

Main article: [History of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Europe)

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistoric Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Europe)



[Last Glacial Maximum refugia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Maximum_refugia), c. 20,000 years ago\
&#x20;

&#x20; [Solutrean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solutrean) culture

&#x20; [Epigravettian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigravettian) culture

[^cite_note-Nature-2023-52]



Paleolithic cave paintings from [Lascaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lascaux) in [France](5843419.md) (c. 15,000 BCE)



[Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge) in the United Kingdom (Late Neolithic from 3000 to 2000 BCE)

During the 2.5 million years of the [Pleistocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleistocene), numerous cold phases called [glacials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacial_period) ([Quaternary ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary_glaciation)), or significant advances of continental ice sheets, in Europe and North America, occurred at intervals of approximately 40,000 to 100,000 years. The long glacial periods were separated by more temperate and shorter [interglacials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interglacial) which lasted about 10,000–15,000 years. The last cold episode of the [last glacial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_glacial_period) ended about 10,000 years ago.[^cite_note-53] Earth is currently in an interglacial period of the Quaternary, called the [Holocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocene).[^cite_note-54]

*[Homo erectus georgicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus_georgicus)*, which lived roughly 1.8 million years ago in [Georgia](48768.md), is the earliest [hominin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominini) to have been discovered in Europe.[^cite_note-55] [Other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_antecessor) hominin remains, dating back roughly 1 million years, have been discovered in [Atapuerca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_Site_of_Atapuerca), [Spain](26667.md).[^cite_note-56] [Neanderthal man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal_man) (named after the [Neandertal valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neandertal_\(valley\)) in [Germany](11867.md)) appeared in Europe 150,000 years ago (115,000 years ago it is found already in the territory of present-day [Poland](22936.md)[^cite_note-57]) and disappeared from the fossil record about 40,000 years ago,[^cite_note-58] with their final refuge being the Iberian Peninsula. The Neanderthals were supplanted by modern humans ([Cro-Magnons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cro-Magnons)), who seem to have appeared in Europe around 43,000 to 40,000 years ago.[^cite_note-natgeo_21-59] However, there is also evidence that Homo sapiens arrived in Europe around 54,000 years ago, some 10,000 years earlier than previously thought.[^cite_note-60] The earliest sites in Europe dated 48,000 years ago are [Riparo Mochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riparo_Mochi) (Italy), [Geissenklösterle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geissenkl%C3%B6sterle) (Germany) and [Isturitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isturitz) (France).[^cite_note-range-61][^cite_note-62]

The [European Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Neolithic) period—marked by the cultivation of crops and the raising of livestock, increased numbers of settlements and the widespread use of pottery—began around 7000 BCE in [Greece](12108.md) and the [Balkans](4829.md), probably influenced by earlier farming practices in [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) and the [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East).[^cite_note-Borza-63] It spread from the Balkans along the valleys of the [Danube](21209639.md) and the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine) ([Linear Pottery culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_Pottery_culture)), and along the [Mediterranean coast](19006.md) ([Cardial culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardial_Ware)). Between 4500 and 3000 BCE, these central European neolithic cultures developed further to the west and the north, transmitting newly acquired skills in producing copper artifacts. In Western Europe the Neolithic period was characterised not by large agricultural settlements but by field monuments, such as [causewayed enclosures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causewayed_enclosure), [burial mounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burial_mound) and [megalithic tombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalithic_tomb).[^cite_note-64] The [Corded Ware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corded_Ware) cultural horizon flourished at the transition from the Neolithic to the [Chalcolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcolithic). During this period giant [megalithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalithic) monuments, such as the [Megalithic Temples of Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalithic_Temples_of_Malta) and [Stonehenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonehenge), were constructed throughout Western and Southern Europe.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66]

The modern native populations of Europe largely descend from three distinct lineages:[^cite_note-Indo-European-67] Mesolithic [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer), descended from populations associated with the Paleolithic [Epigravettian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigravettian) culture;[^cite_note-Nature-2023-52] Neolithic [Early European Farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_Farmers) who migrated from Anatolia during the [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution) 9,000 years ago;[^cite_note-68] and [Yamnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamnaya_culture) [Steppe herders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herders) who expanded into Europe from the [Pontic–Caspian steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic%E2%80%93Caspian_steppe) of Ukraine and southern Russia in the context of [Indo-European migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_migrations) 5,000 years ago.[^cite_note-Indo-European-67][^cite_note-69] The [European Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Bronze_Age) began c. 3200 BCE in Greece with the [Minoan civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoan_civilization) on [Crete](6591.md), the first advanced civilisation in Europe.[^cite_note-70] The Minoans were followed by the [Myceneans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenean_Greece), who collapsed suddenly around 1200 BCE, ushering the [European Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Iron_Age).[^cite_note-71] Iron Age colonisation by the [Greeks](42056.md) and [Phoenicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicians) gave rise to early [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_basin) cities. Early [Iron Age Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age_Italy) and [Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_Greece) from around the 8th century BCE gradually gave rise to historical Classical antiquity, whose beginning is sometimes dated to 776 BCE, the year of the first [Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Olympic_Games).[^cite_note-72]

### Classical antiquity

Main article: [Classical antiquity](252905.md)

See also: [Ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and [Ancient Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome)



The [Parthenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenon) in [Athens](1216.md) (432 BCE)

Ancient Greece was the founding culture of Western civilisation. Western [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) and [rationalist culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism) are often attributed to Ancient Greece.[^cite_note-Daly2013-73] The Greek city-state, the [polis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis), was the fundamental political unit of classical Greece.[^cite_note-Daly2013-73] In 508 BCE, [Cleisthenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleisthenes) instituted the world's first [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athenian_democracy) system of government in [Athens](1216.md).[^cite_note-BKDunn1992-74] The Greek political ideals were rediscovered in the late 18th century by European philosophers and idealists. Greece also generated many cultural contributions: in [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy), [humanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanism) and [rationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism) under [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle), [Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socrates) and [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato); in [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography) with [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) and [Thucydides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thucydides); in dramatic and narrative verse, starting with the epic poems of [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer);[^cite_note-natgeo_76-75] in drama with [Sophocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophocles) and [Euripides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euripides); in medicine with [Hippocrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippocrates) and [Galen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galen); and in science with [Pythagoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythagoras), [Euclid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclid), and [Archimedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archimedes).[^cite_note-Heath-76][^cite_note-Heath_Vol_2-77][^cite_note-78] In the course of the 5th century BCE, several of the Greek [city states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_states) would ultimately check the [Achaemenid Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) advance in Europe through the [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars), considered a pivotal moment in world history,[^cite_note-Strauss2005-79] as the 50 years of peace that followed are known as [Golden Age of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth-century_Athens), the seminal period of ancient Greece that laid many of the foundations of Western civilisation.



Animation showing the growth and division of [Ancient Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome)

Greece was followed by [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome), which left its mark on [law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law), [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics), [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), [engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_engineering), [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_architecture), [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centralized_government), and many more key aspects in western civilisation.[^cite_note-Daly2013-73] By 200 BCE, Rome had conquered [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Italy) and over the following two centuries it conquered [Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), [Hispania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispania) ([Spain](26667.md) and [Portugal](23033.md)), the [North African](21714.md) coast, much of the [Middle East](19323.md), [Gaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaul) ([France](5843419.md) and [Belgium](3343.md)), and [Britannia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain) (England and [Wales](69894.md)).

Expanding from their base in central Italy beginning in the third century BCE, the Romans gradually expanded to eventually rule the entire Mediterranean basin and Western Europe by the turn of the millennium. The [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) ended in 27 BCE, when [Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus) proclaimed the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire). The two centuries that followed are known as the *[pax romana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_romana)*, a period of unprecedented peace, prosperity and political stability in most of Europe.[^cite_note-mieawl-80] The empire continued to expand under emperors such as [Antoninus Pius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoninus_Pius) and [Marcus Aurelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Aurelius), who spent time on the Empire's northern border fighting [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples), [Pictish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picts) and [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people) tribes.[^cite_note-natgeo_123-81][^cite_note-82] [Christianity](5211.md) was [legalised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great_and_Christianity) by [Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I) in 313 CE after three centuries of [imperial persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_early_Christians_in_the_Roman_Empire). Constantine also permanently moved the capital of the empire from Rome to the city of [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium) (modern-day [Istanbul](3391396.md)) which was renamed [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) in his honour in 330 CE. Christianity became the sole official religion of the empire in 380 CE, and in 391–392 CE the emperor [Theodosius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodosius_I) outlawed pagan religions.[^cite_note-FriellWilliams2005-83] This is sometimes considered to mark the end of antiquity; alternatively antiquity is considered to end with the [fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) in 476 CE; the closure of the pagan [Platonic Academy of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platonic_Academy) in 529 CE;[^cite_note-84] or the rise of Islam in the early 7th century CE. During most of its existence, the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) was one of the most powerful economic, cultural, and military forces in Europe.[^cite_note-85]

### Early Middle Ages

Main articles: [Late Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Antiquity) and [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages)

See also: [Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_Ages_\(historiography\)) and [Age of Migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Migrations)



Europe c. 650



[Charlemagne's empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_Empire) in 814:      Francia,      Tributaries

During the [decline of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_the_Roman_Empire), Europe entered a long period of change arising from what historians call the "[Age of Migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Migrations)". There were numerous invasions and migrations amongst the [Ostrogoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostrogoths), [Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigoths), [Goths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths), [Vandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandals), [Huns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huns), [Franks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franks), [Angles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angles_\(tribe\)), [Saxons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxons), [Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs), [Avars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Avars), [Bulgars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgars), [Vikings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikings), [Pechenegs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechenegs), [Cumans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumans), and [Magyars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magyars).[^cite_note-mieawl-80] [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) thinkers such as [Petrarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrarch) would later refer to this as the "Dark Ages".[^cite_note-86]

Isolated monastic communities were the only places to safeguard and compile written knowledge accumulated previously; apart from this, very few written records survive. Much literature, philosophy, mathematics, and other thinking from the classical period disappeared from Western Europe, though they were preserved in the east, in the Byzantine Empire.[^cite_note-87]

While the Roman empire in the west continued to decline, Roman traditions and the Roman state remained strong in the predominantly Greek-speaking territories in the east, known as the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). During most of its existence, the Byzantine Empire was the most powerful economic, cultural, and military force in Europe. Emperor [Justinian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I) presided over Constantinople's first golden age: he established a [legal code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Justinian) that forms the basis of many modern legal systems, funded the construction of the [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia) and brought the Christian church under state control.[^cite_note-natgeo_135-88]

From the 7th century onwards, as the Byzantines and neighbouring [Sasanid Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanids) were severely weakened due to the protracted, centuries-lasting and frequent [Byzantine–Sasanian wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_wars), the Muslim Arabs began to make inroads into historically Roman territory, taking the Levant and North Africa and making inroads into [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor). In the mid-7th century, following the [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia), Islam penetrated into the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) region.[^cite_note-89] Over the next centuries Muslim forces took [Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprus_in_the_Middle_Ages), [Malta](19137.md), [Crete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Crete), [Sicily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Sicily), and [parts of southern Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Islam_in_southern_Italy).[^cite_note-90] Between 711 and 720, most of the lands of the [Visigothic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigothic_Kingdom) of [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) were brought under [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) rule—save for small areas in the northwest ([Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asturias)) and largely [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_people) regions in the [Pyrenees](24707.md). This territory, under the Arabic name [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus), became part of the expanding [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate). The unsuccessful [second siege of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(717%E2%80%93718\)) (717) weakened the [Umayyad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad) and reduced their prestige. The Umayyads were then defeated by the [Frankish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francia) leader [Charles Martel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Martel) at the [Battle of Poitiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tours) in 732, which ended their northward advance. In the remote regions of north-western Iberia and the middle Pyrenees the power of the Muslims in the south was scarcely felt. It was here that the foundations of the Christian kingdoms of [Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Asturias), [Leon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Leon), and [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Galicia) were laid and from where the reconquest of the Iberian Peninsula would start. However, no coordinated attempt would be made to drive the [Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moors) out. The Christian kingdoms were mainly focused on their own internal power struggles. As a result, the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista) took the greater part of eight hundred years, in which period a long list of Alfonsos, Sanchos, Ordoños, Ramiros, Fernandos, and Bermudos would be fighting their Christian rivals as much as the Muslim invaders.



[Viking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking) raids and division of the Frankish Empire at the [Treaty of Verdun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Verdun) in 843



[Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne) is called the "Father of Europe" by many historians for his influence on the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) and on the territory he ruled in Europe. He is seen as a founding figure by multiple [European states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Europe), and several historical [royal houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynasty) of Europe trace their lineage back to him. Charlemagne has been the subject of artworks, monuments and literature during and after the medieval period.

During the Dark Ages, the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire) fell under the control of various tribes. The Germanic and Slav tribes established their domains over Western and Eastern Europe, respectively.[^cite_note-natgeo_143-91] Eventually the Frankish tribes were united under [Clovis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis_I).[^cite_note-natgeo_162-92] [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne), a Frankish king of the [Carolingian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian) dynasty who had conquered most of Western Europe, was anointed "[Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor)" by the Pope in 800. This led to the founding of the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire) in 962, which eventually became centred on the German principalities of central Europe.[^cite_note-natgeo_166-93]

[East Central Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Central_Europe) saw the creation of the first Slavic states and the adoption of [Christianity](5211.md) (c. 1000 CE). The powerful [West Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Slavs) state of [Great Moravia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Moravia) spread its territory all the way south to the Balkans, reaching its largest territorial extent under [Svatopluk I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svatopluk_I_of_Moravia) and causing a series of armed conflicts with [East Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Francia). Further south, the first [South Slavic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavs) emerged in the late 7th and 8th century and adopted Christianity: the [Bulgarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire), the [Serbian Principality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Serbia_\(early_medieval\)) (later [Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia_\(medieval\)) and [Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Empire)), and the [Duchy of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Croatia) (later [Kingdom of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Croatia_\(925%E2%80%931102\))). To the east, [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus') expanded from its capital in [Kiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiev) to become the largest state in Europe by the 10th century. In 988, [Vladimir the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_the_Great) adopted [Orthodox Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) as the religion of state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBullietCrossleyHeadrickHirsch2011250-94][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrownAnatoliosPalmer200966-95] Further east, [Volga Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_Bulgaria) became an Islamic state in the 10th century, but was eventually absorbed into Russia several centuries later.[^cite_note-96]

### High and Late Middle Ages

Main articles: [High Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Middle_Ages), [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages), and [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages)

See also: [Medieval demography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_demography)



The [maritime republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_republics) of medieval [Italy](14532.md) reestablished contacts between Europe, Asia and Africa with extensive trade networks and colonies across the Mediterranean, and had an essential role in the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades).

[^cite_note-97]

[^cite_note-98]

The period between the year 1000 and 1250 is known as the [High Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Middle_Ages), followed by the [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages) until c. 1500.

During the High Middle Ages the population of Europe experienced significant growth, culminating in the [Renaissance of the 12th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_of_the_12th_century). Economic growth, together with the lack of safety on the mainland trading routes, made possible the development of major commercial routes along the coast of the [Mediterranean](19006.md) and [Baltic Seas](3335.md). The growing wealth and independence acquired by some coastal cities gave the [Maritime Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Republics) a leading role in the European scene.

The Middle Ages on the mainland were dominated by the two upper echelons of the social structure: the nobility and the clergy. [Feudalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) developed in [France](5843419.md) in the Early Middle Ages, and soon spread throughout Europe.[^cite_note-natgeo_158-99] A struggle for influence between the [nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobility) and the [monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy) in England led to the writing of [Magna Carta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magna_Carta) and the establishment of a [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament).[^cite_note-natgeo_186-100] The primary source of culture in this period came from the [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church). Through monasteries and [cathedral schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_school), the Church was responsible for education in much of Europe.[^cite_note-natgeo_158-99]



[Tancred of Sicily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tancred_of_Sicily) and [Philip II of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_France), during the [Third Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Crusade) (1189–1192)

The [Papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papacy) reached the height of its power during the High Middle Ages. An [East-West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East-West_Schism) in 1054 split the former Roman Empire religiously, with the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) in the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) and the Roman Catholic Church in the former Western Roman Empire. In 1095 [Pope Urban II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Urban_II) called for a [crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) against [Muslims](19541.md) occupying [Jerusalem](16043.md) and the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land).[^cite_note-natgeo_192-101] In Europe itself, the Church organised the [Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisition) against heretics. In the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula), the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista) concluded with the [fall of Granada in 1492](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granada_War#Last_stand_at_Granada), ending over seven centuries of Islamic rule in the south-western peninsula.[^cite_note-natgeo_199-102]

In the east, a resurgent Byzantine Empire recaptured Crete and Cyprus from the Muslims, and reconquered the Balkans. Constantinople was the largest and wealthiest city in Europe from the 9th to the 12th centuries, with a population of approximately 400,000.[^cite_note-103] The Empire was weakened following the defeat at [Manzikert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manzikert), and was weakened considerably by the [sack of Constantinople in 1204](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(1204\)), during the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade).[^cite_note-DuikerSpielvogel2010-104][^cite_note-Findlay2006-105][^cite_note-Browning1992-106][^cite_note-Byfield2008-107][^cite_note-Golna2004-108][^cite_note-Powell2001-109][^cite_note-Irvin2002-110][^cite_note-Frucht2004-111][^cite_note-DuikerSpielvogel2010v2-112] Although it would recover Constantinople in 1261, [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) [fell in 1453](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) when [Constantinople was taken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) by the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md).[^cite_note-natgeo_211-113][^cite_note-Peters2006-114][^cite_note-Chronicles-115]



The sacking of [Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzdal) by [Batu Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batu_Khan) in 1238, during the [Mongol invasion of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Europe) (1220s–1240s)

In the 11th and 12th centuries, constant incursions by nomadic [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) tribes, such as the [Pechenegs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechenegs) and the [Cuman-Kipchaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuman-Kipchak_Confederation), caused a massive migration of [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_peoples) populations to the safer, heavily forested regions of the north, and temporarily halted the expansion of Kievan Rus' to the south and east.[^cite_note-Klyuch1-116] The [Mongols invaded Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Europe) in the mid-13th century, and together with their mostly Turkic subjects, became known as [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatar_people).[^cite_note-117] These Tatars ruled the [Russian principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_principalities) through vassalage for the next 200 years.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] After the collapse of Mongol dominions, the first Romanian states (principalities) emerged in the 14th century: [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia) and [Walachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walachia). Previously, these territories were under the successive control of Pechenegs and Cumans.[^cite_note-120] The [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow) became the dominant Russian principality, forming in the process a centralized, autocratic Russian state by the late 15th century.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-Dukes-122] The [Great Stand on the Ugra River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Stand_on_the_Ugra_River) in 1480 traditionally marks the end of the "Tatar yoke" over Russia.[^cite_note-Dukes-122]

The [Great Famine of 1315–1317](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_of_1315%E2%80%931317) was the first [crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_of_the_Late_Middle_Ages) that would strike Europe in the late Middle Ages.[^cite_note-123] The period between 1348 and 1420 witnessed the heaviest loss. The population of [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_in_the_Middle_Ages) was reduced by half.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] Medieval Britain was afflicted by 95 famines,[^cite_note-126] and France suffered the effects of 75 or more in the same period.[^cite_note-127] Europe was devastated in the mid-14th century by the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death), one of the most deadly [pandemics](24255.md) in human history which killed an estimated 25 million people in Europe alone—a third of the [European population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_demography) at the time.[^cite_note-128]

The plague had a devastating effect on Europe's social structure; it induced people to live for the moment as illustrated by [Giovanni Boccaccio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Boccaccio) in *[The Decameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Decameron)* (1353). It was a serious blow to the Roman Catholic Church and led to increased [persecution of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews), [beggars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beggars) and [lepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leper).[^cite_note-natgeo_223-129] The plague is thought to have returned every generation with varying [virulence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virulence) and mortalities until the 18th century.[^cite_note-130] During this period, more than 100 plague [epidemics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_epidemics) swept across Europe.[^cite_note-Revill-131]

### Early modern period

Main article: [Early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period)

See also: [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance), [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation), [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution), and [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery)



*[The School of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_School_of_Athens)* (1511) by [Raphael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael): Contemporaries of the artist, including [Leonardo da Vinci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci) and [Michelangelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelangelo), are represented in classical guise in this work emblematic of [Renaissance humanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_humanism).

The Renaissance was a period of cultural change originating in [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence), and later spreading to the rest of Europe. The rise of a [new humanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_humanism) was accompanied by the recovery of forgotten [classical Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece) and Arabic knowledge from [monastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastic) libraries, often translated from Arabic into [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language).[^cite_note-Barrett-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] The Renaissance spread across Europe between the 14th and 16th centuries: it saw the flowering of [art](311401.md), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), and [the sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science_in_the_Renaissance), under the joint patronage of [royalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_family), the nobility, the [Catholic Church](606848.md) and an emerging merchant class.[^cite_note-natgeo_254-135][^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] Patrons in Italy, including the [Medici](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medici) family of Florentine bankers and the [popes](23056.md) in [Rome](25458.md), funded prolific [quattrocento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quattrocento) and [cinquecento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinquecento) artists such as [Raphael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael), [Michelangelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelangelo) and [Leonardo da Vinci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci).[^cite_note-natgeo_292-138][^cite_note-139]

Political intrigue within the Church in the mid-14th century caused the [Western Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Schism). During this 40-year period, two popes—one in [Avignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avignon) and one in Rome—claimed rulership over the Church. Although the schism was eventually healed in 1417, the papacy's spiritual authority had suffered greatly.[^cite_note-natgeo_193-140] In the 15th century, Europe started to extend itself beyond its geographic frontiers. Spain and Portugal, the greatest naval powers of the time, took the lead in exploring the world.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-natgeo_296-142] Exploration reached the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere) in the Atlantic and the southern tip of Africa. [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus) reached the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World) in 1492, and [Vasco da Gama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama) opened the ocean route to the East, linking the Atlantic and [Indian Oceans](14580.md) in 1498. The Portuguese-born explorer [Ferdinand Magellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Magellan) reached Asia westward across the Atlantic and the [Pacific Oceans](23070.md) in a Spanish expedition, resulting in the first [circumnavigation of the globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Magellan's_circumnavigation), completed by the Spaniard [Juan Sebastián Elcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Sebasti%C3%A1n_Elcano) (1519–1522). Soon after, the Spanish and Portuguese began establishing large global empires in the [Americas](29833.md), Asia, Africa and Oceania.[^cite_note-natgeo_338-143] France, the [Netherlands](21148.md) and England soon followed in building large colonial empires with vast holdings in Africa, the Americas and Asia. In 1588, the [Spanish Armada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Armada) failed to invade England. A year later, [England tried unsuccessfully to invade Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Armada), allowing [Philip II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain) to maintain his dominant war capacity in Europe. This English disaster also allowed the Spanish fleet to retain its capability to wage war for the next decades. However, two more Spanish armadas failed to invade England ([2nd Spanish Armada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Spanish_Armada) and [3rd Spanish Armada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_Spanish_Armada)).[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147]



[Habsburg dominions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg) in the centuries following their partition by [Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor). The principal military base of Philip II in Europe was the Spanish road stretching from the Netherlands to the [Duchy of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Milan).

[^cite_note-148]

The Church's power was further weakened by the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation), which began in 1517 when German theologian [Martin Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther) nailed his *[Ninety-five Theses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-five_Theses)* criticising the selling of indulgences to the church door. He was subsequently excommunicated in the papal bull *[Exsurge Domine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exsurge_Domine)* in 1520 and his followers were condemned in the 1521 [Diet of Worms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diet_of_Worms), which divided German princes between [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) and Catholic faiths.[^cite_note-natgeo_256-149] [Religious fighting and warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_wars_of_religion) spread with Protestantism.[^cite_note-150] The plunder of the empires of the Americas allowed Spain to finance [religious persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition) in Europe for over a century.[^cite_note-151] The [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War) (1618–1648) crippled the Holy Roman Empire and devastated much of Germany, killing between 25 and 40 percent of its population.[^cite_note-152] In the aftermath of the [Peace of Westphalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Westphalia), France rose to predominance within Europe.[^cite_note-natgeo_269-153] The defeat of the [Ottoman Turks](22278.md) at the [Battle of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vienna) in 1683 marked the historic end of [Ottoman expansion into Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_wars_in_Europe).[^cite_note-154]

In much of Central and Eastern Europe, the 17th century was [a period of general decline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_General_Crisis);[^cite_note-155] the region experienced more than 150 famines in a 200-year period between 1501 and 1700.[^cite_note-156] From the [Union of Krewo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Krewo) (1385) east-central Europe was dominated by the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland_\(1385%E2%80%931569\)) and the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania). The hegemony of the vast [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth) had ended with the devastation brought by the [Northern War of 1655–1660](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_War_of_1655%E2%80%931660) ([Deluge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Deluge)) and subsequent conflicts;[^cite_note-157] the state itself was [partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland) and ceased to exist at the end of the 18th century.[^cite_note-158]

From the 15th to 18th centuries, when the disintegrating khanates of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) were conquered by [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia), [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) from the [Crimean Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate) frequently [raided Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean%E2%80%93Nogai_slave_raids_in_Eastern_Europe) to [capture slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_Ottoman_Empire).[^cite_note-159] Further east, the [Nogai Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nogai_Horde) and [Kazakh Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate) frequently raided the Slavic-speaking areas of contemporary Russia and Ukraine for hundreds of years, until the Russian expansion and conquest of most of northern Eurasia (i.e. Eastern Europe, Central Asia and Siberia).

The Renaissance and the [New Monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Monarchs) marked the start of an Age of Discovery, a period of exploration, invention and scientific development.[^cite_note-160] Important figures of the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution) during the 16th and 17th centuries included [Copernicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copernicus), [Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler), [Galileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo), and [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton).[^cite_note-161] According to Peter Barrett, "It is widely accepted that 'modern science' arose in the Europe of the 17th century (towards the end of the Renaissance), introducing a new understanding of the natural world."[^cite_note-Barrett-132]

### 18th and 19th centuries

Main article: [Modern history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_history)

See also: [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution), and [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment)



The national boundaries within Europe set by the [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna)

The [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War) brought to an end the ["Old System" of alliances in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Alliance_\(League_of_Augsburg\)). Consequently, when the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) turned into a global war between 1778 and 1783, Britain found itself opposed by a strong coalition of European powers, and lacking any substantial ally.[^cite_note-162]

The Age of Enlightenment was a powerful intellectual movement during the 18th century promoting scientific and reason-based thoughts.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-natgeo_255-165] Discontent with the aristocracy and clergy's monopoly on political power in France resulted in the French Revolution, and the establishment of the [First Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_First_Republic) as a result of which the monarchy and many of the nobility perished during the initial [reign of terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reign_of_Terror).[^cite_note-166] [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) Bonaparte rose to power in the aftermath of the French Revolution, and established the [First French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_Empire) that, during the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars), grew to encompass large parts of Europe before collapsing in 1815 with the [Battle of Waterloo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo).[^cite_note-natgeo_360-167][^cite_note-168] [Napoleonic rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Empire) resulted in the further dissemination of the ideals of the French Revolution, including that of the [nation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state), as well as the widespread adoption of the French models of [administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centralised_government), [law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code) and [education](379033.md).[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-natgeo_350-171] The [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna), convened after Napoleon's downfall, established a new [balance of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_power_\(international_relations\)) in Europe centred on the five "[great powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power)": the UK, France, [Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussia), [Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire), and Russia.[^cite_note-natgeo_367-172] This balance would remain in place until the [Revolutions of 1848](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1848), during which liberal uprisings affected all of Europe except for Russia and the UK. These revolutions were eventually put down by conservative elements and few reforms resulted.[^cite_note-natgeo_371-173] The year 1859 saw the unification of Romania, as a nation state, from smaller principalities. In 1867, the [Austro-Hungarian empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_empire) was [formed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ausgleich); 1871 saw the unifications of both [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_unification) and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Germany) as nation-states from smaller principalities.[^cite_note-174]

In parallel, the [Eastern Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Question) grew more complex ever since the Ottoman defeat in the [Russo-Turkish War (1768–1774)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1768%E2%80%931774\)). As the dissolution of the Ottoman Empire seemed imminent, the [Great Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Power) struggled to safeguard their strategic and commercial interests in the Ottoman domains. The [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) stood to benefit from the decline, whereas the [Habsburg Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy) and Britain perceived the preservation of the Ottoman Empire to be in their best interests. Meanwhile, the [Serbian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Revolution) (1804) and [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence) (1821) marked the beginning of the end of Ottoman rule in the [Balkans](4829.md), which ended with the [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars) in 1912–1913.[^cite_note-175] Formal recognition of the *de facto* independent principalities of [Montenegro](20760.md), [Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Serbia) and [Romania](25445.md) ensued at the [Congress of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Berlin) in 1878, when [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Bulgaria) also gained *de facto* independence.



Marshall's [Temple Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_Works) (1840); the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) started in [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain).

The [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) began in [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) in the last part of the 18th century and spread throughout Europe. The invention and implementation of new technologies resulted in rapid urban growth, mass employment and the rise of a new working class.[^cite_note-176] Reforms in social and economic spheres followed, including the [first laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factory_Acts) on [child labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_labour), the legalisation of [trade unions](17626.md),[^cite_note-177] and the [abolition of slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-178] In Britain, the [Public Health Act 1875](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_Act_1875) was passed, which significantly improved living conditions in many British cities.[^cite_note-179] Europe's population increased from about 100 million in 1700 to 400 million by 1900.[^cite_note-180] The last major famine recorded in Western Europe, the [Great Famine of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_\(Ireland\)), caused death and mass emigration of millions of Irish people.[^cite_note-181] In the 19th century, 70 million people left Europe in migrations to various European colonies abroad and to the United States.[^cite_note-182] The industrial revolution also led to large population growth, and the [share of the world population living in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_world#Shares_of_world_population,_AD_1%E2%80%931998_\(%_of_world_total\)) reached a peak of slightly above 25% around the year 1913.[^cite_note-ggdc.net-183][^cite_note-World_Population_Growth,_1950–2050-184]

### 20th century to the present
