Main articles: [Modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_era) and [History of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Europe)

See also: [World War I](4764461.md), [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), [Interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period), [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War), [Cold War](325329.md), and [History of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_European_Union)



Map of European [colonial empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_empire) throughout the world in 1914

Two world wars and an economic depression dominated the first half of the 20th century. The First World War was fought between 1914 and 1918. It started when [Archduke Franz Ferdinand of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archduke_Franz_Ferdinand_of_Austria) was assassinated by the [Yugoslav nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_nationalism)[^cite_note-185] [Gavrilo Princip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavrilo_Princip).[^cite_note-natgeo_407-186] Most European nations were drawn into the war, which was fought between the [Entente Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entente_Powers) ([France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Third_Republic), the [United Kingdom](31717.md), [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire), [Belgium](3343.md), [Serbia](29265.md), and later [Italy](14532.md), [Greece](12108.md), [Romania](25445.md), Portugal, and the United States) and the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) ([Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire), [Bulgaria](3415.md), and the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md)). The war left more than 16 million civilians and military dead.[^cite_note-natgeo_440-187] Over 60 million European soldiers were mobilised from 1914 to 1918.[^cite_note-188]



Map depicting the military alliances of the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War) in 1914–1918

Russia was plunged into the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution), which threw down the [Tsarist monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and replaced it with the [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist) [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union),[^cite_note-natgeo_480-189] leading also to the independence of many former [Russian governorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorate_\(Russia\)), such as [Finland](10577.md), [Estonia](28222445.md), [Latvia](17514.md) and [Lithuania](17675.md), as new European countries.[^cite_note-190] [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) and the Ottoman Empire collapsed and broke up into separate nations, and many other nations had their borders redrawn. The [Treaty of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles), which officially ended the First World War in 1919, was harsh towards Germany, upon whom it placed full responsibility for the war and imposed heavy sanctions.[^cite_note-natgeo_443-191] Excess deaths in Russia over the course of the First World War and the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War) (including the postwar [famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_famine_of_1921)) amounted to a combined total of 18 million.[^cite_note-192] In 1932–1933, under [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalin)'s leadership, confiscations of grain by the Soviet authorities contributed to the [second Soviet famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_famine_of_1932-1933) which caused millions of deaths;[^cite_note-193] surviving [kulaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kulak) were persecuted and many sent to [Gulags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag) to do [forced labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unfree_labour). Stalin was also responsible for the [Great Purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Purge) of 1937–38 in which the [NKVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NKVD) executed 681,692 people;[^cite_note-194] millions of people were [deported and exiled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) to remote areas of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-195]



[Serbian war efforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Campaign_of_World_War_I) (1914–1918) cost the country one quarter of its population.

[^cite_note-196]

[^cite_note-197]

[^cite_note-198]

[^cite_note-199]

[^cite_note-200]



[Nazi Germany](21212.md) began the devastating Second World War in Europe by its leader, [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md). Here Hitler, on the right, with his closest ally, the Italian dictator [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini), in 1940.

The [social revolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_revolution) sweeping through Russia also affected other European nations following [The Great War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_War): in 1919, with the [Weimar Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar_Republic) in Germany and the [First Austrian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Austrian_Republic); in 1922, with [Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini)'s one-party [fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) government in the [Kingdom of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy) and in [Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk)'s [Turkish Republic](11125639.md), adopting the Western alphabet and state [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism). Economic instability, caused in part by debts incurred in the First World War and 'loans' to Germany played havoc in Europe in the late 1920s and 1930s. This, and the [Wall Street crash of 1929](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_crash_of_1929), brought about the worldwide [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression). Helped by the economic crisis, social instability and the threat of communism, [fascist movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) developed throughout Europe placing [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) in power of what became [Nazi Germany](21212.md).[^cite_note-hobsbawn-201][^cite_note-natgeo_438-202]

In 1933, Hitler became the leader of Germany and began to work towards his goal of building Greater Germany. Germany re-expanded and took back the [Saarland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saarland) and [Rhineland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhineland) in 1935 and 1936. In 1938, [Austria](26964606.md) became a part of Germany following the [Anschluss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anschluss). Following the [Munich Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich_Agreement) signed by Germany, France, the United Kingdom, and Italy, later in 1938 Germany annexed the [Sudetenland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudetenland), which was a part of [Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czechoslovakia) inhabited by ethnic Germans. In early 1939, the remainder of Czechoslovakia was split into the [Protectorate of Bohemia and Moravia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate_of_Bohemia_and_Moravia), controlled by Germany and the [Slovak Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovak_Republic_\(1939%E2%80%931945\)). At the time, the United Kingdom and France preferred a policy of [appeasement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeasement).

With tensions mounting between Germany and [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Polish_Republic) over the future of [Danzig](12099.md), the Germans turned to the Soviets and signed the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact), which allowed the Soviets to invade the Baltic states and parts of Poland and Romania. Germany [invaded Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland) on 1 September 1939, prompting France and the United Kingdom to declare war on Germany on 3 September, opening the [European theatre of the Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_theatre_of_World_War_II).[^cite_note-reich-203][^cite_note-natgeo_465-204][^cite_note-205] The [Soviet invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Poland) started on 17 September and Poland fell soon thereafter. On 24 September, the Soviet Union attacked the [Baltic countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_the_Baltic_states_\(1940\)) and, on 30 November, Finland, the latter of which was followed by the devastating [Winter War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_War) for the Red Army.[^cite_note-206] The British hoped to land at [Narvik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_Narvik) and send troops to aid Finland, but their primary objective in the landing was to encircle Germany and cut the Germans off from Scandinavian resources. Around the same time, Germany moved troops into Denmark. The [Phoney War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoney_War) continued.

In May 1940, Germany [attacked France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_France) through the Low Countries. France capitulated in June 1940. By August, Germany had begun a [bombing offensive against the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Britain) but failed to convince the Britons to give up.[^cite_note-natgeo_510-207] In 1941, Germany invaded the Soviet Union in [Operation Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa).[^cite_note-natgeo_532-208] On 7 December 1941 [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan)'s [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor) drew the United States into the conflict as allies of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire), and other [allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) forces.[^cite_note-natgeo_511-209][^cite_note-natgeo_519-210]



The "[Big Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II)" at the [Yalta Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Conference) in 1945; seated (from the left): [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill), [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) and [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)

After the staggering [Battle of Stalingrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Stalingrad) in 1943, the German offensive in the Soviet Union turned into a continual fallback. The [Battle of Kursk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kursk), which involved the largest [tank battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Prokhorovka) in history, was the last major German offensive on the [Eastern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)). In June 1944, British and American forces invaded France in the [D-Day landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings), opening a new front against Germany. Berlin finally [fell in 1945](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Berlin), ending the Second World War in Europe. The war was the largest and most destructive in human history, with [60 million dead across the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties).[^cite_note-natgeo_439-211] More than 40 million people in Europe had died as a result of the Second World War,[^cite_note-212] including between 11 and 17 million people who perished during [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-213] The Soviet Union [lost around 27 million people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union) (mostly civilians) during the war, about half of all Second World War casualties.[^cite_note-214] By the end of the Second World War, Europe had more than 40 million [refugees](45547.md).[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] Several [post-war expulsions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_evacuation_and_expulsion) in Central and Eastern Europe displaced a total of about 20 million people.[^cite_note-218]

The First World War, and especially the Second World War, diminished the eminence of Western Europe in world affairs. After the Second World War the map of Europe was redrawn at the [Yalta Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Conference) and divided into two blocs, the Western countries and the communist Eastern bloc, separated by what was later called by [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill) an "[Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain)". The United States and Western Europe established the [NATO](21133.md) alliance and, later, the Soviet Union and Central Europe established the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact).[^cite_note-natgeo_530-219] Particular hot spots after the Second World War were [Berlin](3354.md) and [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste), whereby the [Free Territory of Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Territory_of_Trieste), founded in 1947 with the UN, was dissolved in 1954 and 1975, respectively. The [Berlin blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_blockade) in 1948 and 1949 and the construction of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall) in 1961 were one of the great international crises of the [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222]

The two new [superpowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superpower), the United States and the Soviet Union, became locked in a fifty-year-long Cold War, centred on [nuclear proliferation](22158.md). At the same time [decolonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation), which had already started after the First World War, gradually resulted in the independence of most of the European colonies in Asia and Africa.[^cite_note-natgeo_534-14]



[Flag of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Europe), adopted by the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) in 1955 as the flag for the whole of Europe

[^cite_note-223]

In the 1980s the [reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost) of [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) and the [Solidarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)) movement in Poland weakened the previously rigid communist system. The opening of the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain) at the [Pan-European Picnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_Picnic) then set in motion a peaceful chain reaction, at the end of which the [Eastern bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_bloc), the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) and other [communist states collapsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989), and the Cold War ended.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] Germany was reunited, after the symbolic [fall of the Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall#Fall_of_the_Wall) in 1989 and the maps of Central and Eastern Europe were redrawn once more.[^cite_note-227] This made old previously interrupted cultural and economic relationships possible, and previously isolated cities such as [Berlin](3354.md), [Prague](23844.md), [Vienna](55866.md), [Budapest](36787.md) and [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste) were now again in the centre of Europe.[^cite_note-hobsbawn-201][^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]

[European integration](683704.md) also grew after the Second World War. In 1949 the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) was founded, following a speech by Sir [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill), with the idea of unifying Europe[^cite_note-europaeu_1945-59-15] to achieve common goals. It includes all European states except for [Belarus](3457.md), Russia,[^cite_note-231] and [Vatican City](32408.md). The [Treaty of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Rome) in 1957 established the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community) between six Western European states with the goal of a unified economic policy and common market.[^cite_note-natgeo_536-232] The 1967 [Merger Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merger_Treaty) created a single Commission and Council for the [European Economic Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Community), the [European Coal and Steel Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Coal_and_Steel_Community), and [Euratom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euratom), while the three communities remained legally distinct.[^cite_note-233] The [Maastricht Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastricht_Treaty), which entered into force in 1993, established the [European Union](9317.md) and renamed the European Economic Community as the European Community.[^cite_note-234] The EU established [a parliament](9581.md), [a court](87187.md) and [a central bank](9474.md), and introduced the [euro](9472.md) as a unified currency.[^cite_note-natgeo_537-235] Between 2004 and 2013, more Central European countries began joining, [expanding the EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_the_European_Union) to 28 European countries and once more making Europe a major economical and political centre of power.[^cite_note-natgeo_535-236] However, the United Kingdom withdrew from the EU on 31 January 2020, as a result of a [June 2016 referendum on EU membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum).[^cite_note-237] The [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md) steeply escalated after the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) on 24 February 2022, marking the largest humanitarian and refugee crisis in Europe since the Second World War[^cite_note-238] and the [Yugoslav Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars).[^cite_note-239]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Europe)



General topographic map of Europe showing physical, political, and population characteristics, as per 2024

Europe makes up the western fifth of the [Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) landmass.[^cite_note-Encarta-24] It has a higher ratio of coast to landmass than any other continent or subcontinent.[^cite_note-240] Its maritime borders consist of the Arctic Ocean to the north, the Atlantic Ocean to the west and the Mediterranean, Black and Caspian Seas to the south.[^cite_note-241] Land relief in Europe shows great variation within relatively small areas. The southern regions are more mountainous, while moving north the terrain descends from the high [Alps](981.md), [Pyrenees](24707.md) and [Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathians), through hilly uplands, into broad, low northern plains, which are vast in the east. This extended lowland is known as the [Great European Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_European_Plain) and at its heart lies the [North German Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_German_Plain). An arc of uplands also exists along the north-western seaboard, which begins in the western parts of the islands of Britain and Ireland, and then continues along the mountainous, [fjord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fjord)-cut spine of Norway.

This description is simplified. Subregions such as the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) and the [Italian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Peninsula) contain their own complex features, as does mainland Central Europe itself, where the relief contains many plateaus, river valleys and basins that complicate the general trend. Sub-regions like [Iceland](14531.md), Britain and Ireland are special cases. The former is a land unto itself in the northern ocean that is counted as part of Europe, while the latter are upland areas that were once joined to the mainland until [rising sea levels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise) cut them off.

The [largest European lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_lakes_of_Europe) are located in the north of the continent. The [Volga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga) and [Danube](21209639.md) are the two longest [rivers of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Europe). Europe's largest [waterfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterfall) (by [flow rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_waterfalls_by_flow_rate)) are the [Rhine Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine_Falls) on the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine).

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Europe)



[Biomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biome) of Europe and surrounding regions: \
&#x20;     [tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra)      [alpine tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_tundra)      [taiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga)      [montane forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montane_forest) \
&#x20;     [temperate broadleaf forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_broadleaf_forest)      [mediterranean forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_forest)      [temperate steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_steppe)      [dry steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_steppe)&#x20;

Europe lies mainly in the [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate) climate zone of the northern hemisphere, where the [prevailing wind direction is from the west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevailing_westerlies). The climate is milder in comparison to other areas of the same latitude around the globe due to the influence of the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream), an ocean current which carries warm water from the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md) across the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) to Europe.[^cite_note-climate-242] The Gulf Stream is nicknamed "Europe's central heating", because it makes Europe's climate warmer and wetter than it would otherwise be. The Gulf Stream not only carries warm water to Europe's coast but also warms up the prevailing westerly winds that blow across the continent from the Atlantic Ocean.

Therefore, the average temperature throughout the year of [Aveiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aveiro,_Portugal) is 16 °C (61 °F), while it is only 13 °C (55 °F) in [New York City](645042.md) which is almost on the same latitude, bordering the same ocean. Berlin, Germany; Calgary, Canada; and Irkutsk, in far south-eastern Russia, lie on around the same latitude; January temperatures in Berlin average around 8 °C (14 °F) higher than those in Calgary and they are almost 22 °C (40 °F) higher than average temperatures in Irkutsk.[^cite_note-climate-242]

The large water masses of the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md), which equalise the temperatures on an annual and daily average, are also of particular importance. The water of the Mediterranean extends from the [Sahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahara_desert) to the Alpine arc in its northernmost part of the [Adriatic Sea](23275478.md) near [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste).[^cite_note-243]

In general, Europe is not just colder towards the north compared to the south, but it also gets colder from the west towards the east. The climate is more oceanic in the west and less so in the east. This can be illustrated by the following table of average temperatures at locations roughly following the 64th, 60th, 55th, 50th, 45th and 40th [latitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latitudes). None of them is located at high altitude; most of them are close to the sea.

### Climate change

This section is an excerpt from [Climate change in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Europe).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_in_Europe\&action=edit)]



Increase of average yearly temperature (2000–2017) above the 20th century average in selected cities in Europe&#x20;

[^cite_note-246]

[Climate change](5042951.md) has resulted in an increase in temperature of 2.3 °C (4.14 °F) (2022) in Europe compared to pre-industrial levels. Europe is the fastest warming continent in the world.

[^cite_note-247]

&#x20;[Europe's climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Europe) is getting warmer due to [anthropogenic activity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropogenic_greenhouse_gas). According to international climate experts, global temperature rise should not exceed 2 [°C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C2%B0C) to prevent the most dangerous [consequences of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change); without reduction in [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md), this could happen before 2050.

[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Europe_\:5-248]

[^cite_note-249]

&#x20;[Climate change](5042951.md) has implications for all regions of Europe, with the extent and nature of effects varying across the continent.&#x20;

Effects on European countries include warmer weather and increasing frequency and intensity of [extreme weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_weather) such as [heat waves](293270.md), bringing [health risks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_human_health) and effects on ecosystems, for example the [2026 European drought](83908768.md).[^cite_note-250] European countries are major contributors to global greenhouse gas emissions, although the [European Union](9317.md) and governments of several countries have outlined plans to implement [climate change mitigation](2119179.md) and an [energy transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_transition) in the 21st century, the [European Green Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Deal) being one of these.

### Geology

Main article: [Geology of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Europe)

See also: [Geological history of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geological_history_of_Europe)



Surficial geology of Europe

The geological history of Europe traces back to the formation of the [Baltic Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Shield) (Fennoscandia) and the [Sarmatian craton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarmatian_craton), both around 2.25 billion years ago, followed by the [Volgo–Uralia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volgo%E2%80%93Uralia) shield, the three together leading to the [East European craton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_European_craton) (≈ [Baltica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltica)) which became a part of the [supercontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercontinent) [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_\(supercontinent\)). Around 1.1 billion years ago, Baltica and Arctica (as part of the [Laurentia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurentia) block) became joined to [Rodinia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodinia), later resplitting around 550 million years ago to reform as Baltica. Around 440 million years ago [Euramerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euramerica) was formed from Baltica and Laurentia; a further joining with [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana) then leading to the formation of [Pangea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangea). Around 190 million years ago, Gondwana and [Laurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurasia) split apart due to the widening of the Atlantic Ocean. Finally and very soon afterwards, Laurasia itself split up again, into Laurentia (North America) and the Eurasian continent. The land connection between the two persisted for a considerable time, via [Greenland](12118.md), leading to interchange of animal species. From around 50 million years ago, rising and falling sea levels have determined the actual shape of Europe and its connections with continents such as Asia. Europe's present shape dates to the [late Tertiary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Tertiary) period about five million years ago.[^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-251]

The geology of Europe is hugely varied and complex and gives rise to the wide variety of landscapes found across the continent, from the [Scottish Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Highlands) to the rolling [plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain) of Hungary.[^cite_note-252] Europe's most significant feature is the dichotomy between highland and mountainous [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe) and a vast, partially underwater, northern plain ranging from Ireland in the west to the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains) in the east. These two halves are separated by the mountain chains of the [Pyrenees](24707.md) and [Alps](981.md)/[Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains). The northern plains are delimited in the west by the [Scandinavian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_Mountains) and the mountainous parts of the British Isles. Major shallow water bodies submerging parts of the northern plains are the [Celtic Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Sea), the [North Sea](21179.md), the [Baltic Sea](3335.md) complex and [Barents Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barents_Sea).

The northern plain contains the old geological continent of [Baltica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltica) and so may be regarded geologically as the "main continent", while peripheral highlands and mountainous regions in the south and west constitute fragments from various other geological continents. Most of the older geology of western Europe existed as part of the ancient [microcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microcontinent) [Avalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avalonia).

### Flora



Land use map of Europe with arable farmland (yellow), forest (dark green), pasture (light green) and tundra, or bogs, in the north (dark yellow)

Having lived side by side with agricultural peoples for millennia, Europe's animals and plants have been profoundly affected by the presence and activities of humans. With the exception of [Fennoscandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fennoscandia) and northern Russia, few areas of untouched wilderness are currently found in Europe, except for various [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_park).

The main natural vegetation cover in Europe is mixed [forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest). The conditions for growth are very favourable. In the north, the [Gulf Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Stream) and [North Atlantic Drift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Current) warm the continent. Southern Europe has a warm but mild climate. There are frequent summer droughts in this region. Mountain ridges also affect the conditions. Some of these, such as the [Alps](981.md) and the [Pyrenees](24707.md), are oriented east–west and allow the wind to carry large masses of water from the ocean in the interior. Others are oriented south–north ([Scandinavian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_Mountains), [Dinarides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinaric_Alps), [Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains), [Apennines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apennine_Mountains)) and because the rain falls primarily on the side of mountains that is oriented towards the sea, forests grow well on this side, while on the other side, the conditions are much less favourable. Few corners of mainland Europe have not been grazed by [livestock](25160767.md) at some point in time, and the cutting down of the preagricultural forest habitat caused disruption to the original plant and animal ecosystems. The area of [primary forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_forest) in Europe increased at an average annual rate of 191 000 hectare in 1990–2000, 217 000 ha in 2000–2015 and 153 000 ha in 2015–2025.[^cite_note-253]



Floristic regions of Europe and neighbouring areas, according to Wolfgang Frey and Rainer Lösch

Possibly 80 to 90 percent of Europe was once covered by forest.[^cite_note-254] It stretched from the Mediterranean Sea to the Arctic Ocean. Although over half of Europe's original forests disappeared through the centuries of [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation), Europe still has over one quarter of its land area as forest, such as the [broadleaf and mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_broadleaf_and_mixed_forest) forests, [taiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga) of Scandinavia and Russia, mixed [rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainforest) of the Caucasus and the [Cork oak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cork_oak) forests in the western Mediterranean. During recent times, deforestation has been slowed and many trees have been planted. However, in many cases monoculture [plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation) of [conifers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinophyta) have replaced the original mixed natural forest, because these grow quicker. The plantations now cover vast areas of land, but offer poorer habitats for many European forest dwelling species which require a mixture of tree species and diverse forest structure. The amount of natural forest in Western Europe is just 2–3% or less, while in its Western Russia its 5–10%. The European country with the [smallest percentage of forested area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_forest_area) is [Iceland](14531.md) (1%), while the most forested country is Finland (77%).[^cite_note-255]

In temperate Europe, mixed forest with both [broadleaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flowering_plant) and coniferous trees dominate. The most important species in central and western Europe are [beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beech) and [oak](39696.md). In the north, the taiga is a mixed [spruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spruce)–[pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine)–[birch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birch) forest; further north within Russia and extreme northern Scandinavia, the taiga gives way to [tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra) as the Arctic is approached. In the Mediterranean, many [olive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive) trees have been planted, which are very well adapted to its arid climate; [Mediterranean Cypress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupressus_sempervirens) is also widely planted in southern Europe. The semi-arid Mediterranean region hosts much scrub forest. A narrow east–west tongue of Eurasian [grassland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassland) (the [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe)) extends westwards from [Ukraine](31750.md) and southern Russia and ends in Hungary and traverses into taiga to the north.

### Fauna

Main article: [Fauna of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Europe)



[Biogeographic regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogeography) of Europe and bordering regions

Glaciation during the [most recent ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary_glaciation) and the presence of humans affected the distribution of [European fauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Europe). As for the animals, in many parts of Europe most large animals and top [predator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predator) species have been hunted to extinction. The [woolly mammoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolly_mammoth) was extinct before the end of the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) period. Today [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf) ([carnivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivore)) and [bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bear) ([omnivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnivore)) are endangered. Once they were found in most parts of Europe. However, deforestation and hunting caused these animals to withdraw further and further. By the Middle Ages the bears' habitats were limited to more or less inaccessible mountains with sufficient forest cover. Today, the [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_brown_bear) lives primarily in the [Balkan peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan), Scandinavia and Russia; a small number also persist in other countries across Europe (Austria, Pyrenees etc.), but in these areas brown bear populations are fragmented and marginalised because of the destruction of their habitat. In addition, [polar bears](24408.md) may be found on [Svalbard](27441.md), a Norwegian archipelago far north of Scandinavia. The [wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_wolf), the second-largest predator in Europe after the brown bear, can be found primarily in [Central and Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_and_Eastern_Europe) and in the Balkans, with a handful of packs in pockets of [Western Europe](33800.md) (Scandinavia, Spain, etc.).



Once roaming the great temperate forests of Eurasia, [European bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_bison) now live in nature preserves in [Białowieża Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bia%C5%82owie%C5%BCa_Forest), on the border between [Poland](22936.md) and [Belarus](3457.md).

[^cite_note-256]

[^cite_note-257]

Other carnivores include the [European wildcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_wildcat), [red fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_fox) and [arctic fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_fox), the [golden jackal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_jackal), different species of [martens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marten), the [European hedgehog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_hedgehog), different species of reptiles (like snakes such as vipers and grass snakes) and amphibians, as well as different birds ([owls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owl), [hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawk) and other birds of prey).

Important European herbivores are snails, larvae, fish, different birds and mammals, like rodents, deer and [roe deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_deer), boars and living in the mountains, [marmots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmots), [steinbocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steinbocks), [chamois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamois) among others. A number of insects, such as the [small tortoiseshell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_tortoiseshell) butterfly, add to the biodiversity.[^cite_note-258]

Sea creatures are also an important part of European flora and fauna. The sea flora is mainly [phytoplankton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phytoplankton). Important animals that live in European seas are [zooplankton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zooplankton), [molluscs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mollusc), [echinoderms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echinoderm), different [crustaceans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crustacean), [squids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squid) and [octopuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octopuses), fish, [dolphins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolphin) and [whales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whales).

Biodiversity is protected in Europe through the Council of Europe's [Bern Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Conservation_of_European_Wildlife_and_Natural_Habitats), which has also been signed by the [European Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Community) as well as non-European states.

## Politics

Main article: [Politics of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Europe)

See also: [List of sovereign states and dependent territories in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Europe), [International organisations in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisations_in_Europe), [Regions of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Europe), and [European integration](683704.md)



A clickable [Euler diagram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler_diagram) showing the relationships between various European [regional organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_organisation)&#x20;

- [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Supranational_European_Bodies)

- [t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Supranational_European_Bodies)

- [e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Supranational_European_Bodies)



[Democracy in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Europe) according to 2026 [V-Dem Electoral Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices) with dark blue more democratic and dark red less democratic

[^cite_note-j496-259]

The political map of Europe is substantially derived from the re-organisation of Europe following the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) in 1815. The prevalent form of government in Europe is [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy), in most cases in the form of [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic); in 1815, the prevalent form of government was still the [monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchies_in_Europe). Europe's remaining eleven monarchies[^cite_note-260] are [constitutional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy).

[European integration](683704.md) is the process of political, legal, economic (and in some cases social and cultural) integration of European states as it has been pursued by the powers sponsoring the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) since the end of the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War). The [European Union](9317.md) has been the focus of economic integration on the continent since its foundation in 1993. More recently, the [Eurasian Economic Union](27290106.md) has been established as a counterpart comprising former Soviet states.

27 European states are members of the politico-economic European Union, 29 of the border-free [Schengen Area](16565139.md) and 21 of the monetary union [Eurozone](184391.md). Among the smaller European organisations are the [Nordic Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Council), the [Benelux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benelux), the [Baltic Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Assembly), and the [Visegrád Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visegr%C3%A1d_Group).

The least [democratic countries in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_Europe) were [Belarus](3457.md), Russia and [Turkey](11125639.md) in 2025 according to the [V-Dem Democracy indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_indices).[^cite_note-j496-259]

Several dependencies and similar territories with broad autonomy are also found within or close to Europe. This includes [Åland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%85land) (an [autonomous county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Finland) of Finland), two [autonomous territories of the Kingdom of Denmark](11746442.md) (other than Denmark proper), three [Crown Dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Dependencies) and two [British Overseas Territories](207114.md). [Svalbard](27441.md) is also included due to its unique status within Norway, although it is not autonomous. Not included are the three [countries of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) with devolved powers and the two [Autonomous Regions of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Regions_of_Portugal), which despite having a unique degree of autonomy, are not largely self-governing in matters other than international affairs. Areas with little more than a unique tax status, such as the [Canary Islands](5717.md) and [Heligoland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heligoland), are also not included for this reason.

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Europe)

See also: [List of sovereign states in Europe by GDP (nominal)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and [List of sovereign states in Europe by GDP (PPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_in_Europe_by_GDP_\(PPP\))



GDP (PPP) per capita of European countries

| >\$60,000      \$50,000 – \$60,000       \$40,000 – \$50,000      \$30,000 – \$40,000       \$20,000 – \$30,000      \$10,000 – \$20,000 |
| --- |

As a continent, the economy of Europe is currently the largest on Earth and it is the richest region as measured by assets under management with over \$32.7 trillion compared to North America's \$27.1 trillion in 2008.[^cite_note-263] In 2009 Europe remained the wealthiest region. Its \$37.1 trillion in assets under management represented one-third of the world's wealth. It was one of several regions where wealth surpassed its precrisis year-end peak.[^cite_note-264] As with other continents, Europe has a large [wealth gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_gap) among its countries. The richer states tend to be in the [Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Europe) and [West](33800.md) in general, followed by [Central Europe](5188.md), while most economies of [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe) are still reemerging from the [collapse of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Soviet_Union) and the [breakup of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakup_of_Yugoslavia).

The model of the [Blue Banana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Banana) was designed as an economic geographic representation of the respective economic power of the regions, which was further developed into the [Golden Banana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Banana) or Blue Star. The trade between East and West, as well as towards Asia, which had been disrupted for a long time by the two world wars, new borders and the Cold War, increased sharply after 1989. In addition, there is new impetus from the Chinese [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative) across the [Suez Canal](29323.md) towards Africa and Asia.[^cite_note-265]

The European Union, a political entity composed of 27 European states, comprises the [largest single economic area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) in the world. 21 EU [countries](184391.md) share the [euro](9472.md) as a common currency (in addition, 6 non-EU countries also use the euro as currency). Four European countries rank in the top ten of the world's largest [national economies in GDP (PPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)). This includes (ranks according to the [IMF](15251.md)): Russia (4), Germany (6), France (9) and the United Kingdom (10).

Some European countries are much richer than others. The richest in terms of nominal GDP is [Monaco](19261.md) with its US\$185,829 per capita (2018) and the poorest is [Ukraine](31750.md) with its US\$3,659 per capita (2019).[^cite_note-266]

As a whole, Europe's GDP per capita is US\$21,767 according to a 2016 International Monetary Fund assessment.[^cite_note-267]

| 10 | [Romania](25445.md) (2025) | 968,038 |

### Economic history

Industrial growth (1760–1945)

Capitalism has been dominant in the Western world since the end of feudalism.[^cite_note-269] From Britain, it gradually spread throughout Europe.[^cite_note-270] The [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) started in Europe, specifically the United Kingdom in the late 18th century,[^cite_note-271] and the 19th century saw Western Europe industrialise. Economies were disrupted by the First World War, but by the beginning of the Second World War, they had recovered and were having to compete with the growing economic strength of the United States. The Second World War, again, damaged much of Europe's industries.

Cold War (1945–1991)



Fall of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall) in 1989



[Eurozone](184391.md) (blue colour)

After the Second World War the economy of the UK was in a state of ruin,[^cite_note-272] and continued to suffer relative economic decline in the following decades.[^cite_note-273] Italy was also in a poor economic condition but regained a high level of growth by the 1950s. West Germany [recovered quickly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirtschaftswunder) and had doubled production from pre-war levels by the 1950s.[^cite_note-274] France also staged a remarkable comeback enjoying rapid growth and modernisation; later on Spain, under the leadership of [Franco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Franco), also recovered and the nation recorded huge unprecedented economic growth beginning in the 1960s in what is called the [Spanish miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_miracle).[^cite_note-275] The majority of [Central and Eastern European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_and_Eastern_Europe) states came under the control of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) and thus were members of the [Council for Mutual Economic Assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_for_Mutual_Economic_Assistance) (COMECON).[^cite_note-loc-cs-276]

The states which retained a [free-market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-market) system were given a large amount of aid by the United States under the [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan).[^cite_note-277] The western states moved to link their economies together, providing the basis for the EU and increasing cross border trade. This helped them to enjoy rapidly improving economies, while those states in COMECON were struggling in a large part due to the cost of the [Cold War](325329.md). Until 1990, the [European Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Community) was expanded from 6 founding members to 12. The emphasis placed on resurrecting the West German economy led to it overtaking the UK as Europe's largest economy.

Reunification (1991–present)



One of [Kosovo](17391.md)'s main economical sources is [mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining), because it has large reserves of lead, [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc), silver, [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel), [cobalt](24580536.md), copper, iron and [bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite).

[^cite_note-278]

&#x20;Miners at the [Trepča Mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trep%C4%8Da_Mines) in [Mitrovica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitrovica,_Kosovo), Kosovo in 2011.

With the [Fall of Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Communism) in Central and Eastern Europe in 1991, the post-[socialist states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_state) underwent [shock therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_therapy_\(economics\)) measures to liberalise their economies and implement free market reforms. After [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) and West Germany were reunited in 1990, the economy of West Germany struggled as it had to support and largely rebuild the infrastructure of East Germany, while the latter [experienced sudden mass unemployment and plummeting of industrial production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_German_reunification). By the millennium change, the EU dominated the economy of Europe, comprising the five largest European economies of the time: Germany, the United Kingdom, France, Italy, and Spain. In 1999, 12 of the 15 members of the EU joined the [Eurozone](184391.md), replacing their national currencies by the [euro](9472.md).

Figures released by [Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat) in 2009 confirmed that the Eurozone had gone into [recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_2000s_recession_in_Europe) in 2008.[^cite_note-279] It affected much of the region.[^cite_note-280] The [Euro area crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_area_crisis) especially affected Greece, Ireland, Spain and Portugal.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] As a result, measures were taken, especially for Greece, by the leading countries of the Eurozone.[^cite_note-283] The [European Union](9317.md) unemployment rate was 10.3% in 2012. For those aged 15–24 it was 22.4%.[^cite_note-unemployment-284]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Europe)

See also: [List of European countries by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_population), [List of European countries by life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_life_expectancy), and [Ageing of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ageing_of_Europe)



[Population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth) in and around Europe in 2021

[^cite_note-285]

The population of Europe was about 742 million in 2023 according to UN estimates.[^cite_note-UN_WPP-1][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-2] This is slightly more than one ninth of the world's population.[^cnote_v] The [population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density) of Europe (the number of people per area) is the second highest of any continent, behind Asia. The population of Europe is currently slowly decreasing, by about 0.2% per year,[^cite_note-286] because [there are fewer births than deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility). This natural decrease in population is reduced by the fact that more people [migrate to Europe](15497819.md) from other continents than vice versa.

Southern Europe and Western Europe are the regions with the highest average number of elderly people in the world. In 2021, the percentage of people over 65 years old was 21% in Western Europe and Southern Europe, compared to 19% in all of Europe and 10% in the world.[^cite_note-287] Projections suggest that by 2050 Europe will reach 30%.[^cite_note-288] This is caused by the fact that the population has been [having children below replacement level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) since the 1970s. The [United Nations](31769.md) predicts that Europe will decline in population between 2022 and 2050 by −7 per cent, without changing immigration movements.[^cite_note-Results-289]

According to a population projection of the UN Population Division, Europe's population may fall to between 680 and 720 million people by 2050, which would be 7% of the world population at that time.[^cite_note-290] Within this context, significant disparities exist between regions in relation to [fertility rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rates). The average number of [children per female](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_territories_by_fertility_rate) of child-bearing age is 1.52, far below the replacement rate.[^cite_note-291] The UN predicts a steady [population decline](720599.md) in [Central and Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_and_Eastern_Europe) as a result of emigration and low birth rates.[^cite_note-292]

### Ethnic groups

Main article: [Ethnic groups in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe)

Further information: [Genetic history of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Europe)

Pan and Pfeil (2004) count 87 distinct "peoples of Europe", of which 33 form the majority population in at least one sovereign state, while the remaining 54 constitute [ethnic minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_minority).[^cite_note-293] [Romani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) are the largest ethnic minority in Europe, according to [European Commission](9974.md).[^cite_note-294]

### Migration

Main article: [Immigration to Europe](15497819.md)

See also: [European diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_diaspora)

Europe is home to the highest number of migrants of all global regions at nearly 87 million people in 2020, according to the [International Organization for Migration](36893.md).[^cite_note-295] In 2005, the EU had an overall net gain from [immigration](4599312.md) of 1.8 million people. This accounted for almost 85% of Europe's total [population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth).[^cite_note-296] In 2021, 827,000 persons were given citizenship of an EU member state, an increase of about 14% compared with 2020.[^cite_note-eurostatMigration-297] 2.3 million immigrants from non-EU countries entered the EU in 2021.[^cite_note-eurostatMigration-297]

Early modern [emigration from Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_Europe) began with Spanish and Portuguese settlers in the 16th century,[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-ppghis.ifcs.ufrj.br-299] and French and English settlers in the 17th century.[^cite_note-300] But numbers remained relatively small until waves of mass emigration in the 19th century, when millions of poor families left Europe.[^cite_note-301]

Today, [large populations of European descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_diaspora) are found on every continent. European ancestry predominates in North America and to a lesser degree in South America (particularly in [Uruguay](31843.md), [Argentina](18951905.md), [Chile](5489.md) and [Brazil](3383.md), while most of the other [Latin American](18524.md) countries also have a considerable [population of European origins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Latin_American)). Australia and New Zealand have large European-derived populations. Africa has no countries with European-derived majorities (or with the exception of [Cape Verde](18962637.md) and probably [São Tomé and Príncipe](20026306.md), depending on context), but there are significant minorities, such as the [White South Africans](4734206.md) in [South Africa](17416221.md). In Asia, European-derived populations, specifically [Russians](59510.md), predominate in [North Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Asia) and some parts of Northern [Kazakhstan](16642.md).[^cite_note-302] Also in Asia, Europeans, especially the Spanish are an influential [minority population in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Filipinos).[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Europe)

See also: [List of European languages by number of speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_languages_by_number_of_speakers)



Distribution of the major [languages of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Europe)

Europe has about 225 indigenous languages,[^cite_note-305] mostly falling within three [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) language groups: the [Romance languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_languages), derived from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language) of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire); the [Germanic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_languages), whose ancestor language came from southern Scandinavia; and the [Slavic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_languages).[^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-251] Slavic languages are mostly spoken in Southern, Central and Eastern Europe. Romance languages are spoken primarily in Western and Southern Europe, as well as in [Switzerland](26748.md) in Central Europe and [Romania](25445.md) and [Moldova](19260.md) in Eastern Europe. Germanic languages are spoken in Western, Northern and Central Europe as well as in [Gibraltar](7607314.md) and [Malta](19137.md) in Southern Europe.[^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-251] Languages in adjacent areas show significant overlaps (such as in [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_\(language\)), for example). Other Indo-European languages outside the three main groups include the [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_languages) group ([Latvian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latvian_language) and [Lithuanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_language)), the [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_languages) group ([Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_language), [Scottish Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic), [Manx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manx_language), [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language), [Cornish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_language) and [Breton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breton_language)[^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica-251]), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) and [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language).

A distinct non-Indo-European family of [Uralic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_languages) ([Estonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_language), [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language), [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language), [Erzya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erzya_language), [Komi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komi_language), [Mari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari_language), [Moksha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha_language) and [Udmurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Udmurt_language)) is spoken mainly in [Estonia](28222445.md), [Finland](10577.md), [Hungary](13275.md) and parts of Russia. [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages) include [Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_language), [Kazakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_language) and [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), in addition to smaller languages in Eastern and Southeast Europe ([Balkan Gagauz Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Gagauz_Turkish), [Bashkir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashkir_language), [Chuvash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuvash_language), [Crimean Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatar_language), [Karachay-Balkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachay-Balkar_language), [Kumyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumyk_language), [Nogai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nogai_language) and [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatar_language)). [Kartvelian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartvelian_languages) ([Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language), [Mingrelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mingrelian_language) and [Svan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svan_language)) are spoken primarily in [Georgia](48768.md). Two other language families reside in the North Caucasus (termed [Northeast Caucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Caucasian_languages), most notably including [Chechen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chechen_language), [Avar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avar_language) and [Lezgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lezgian_language); and [Northwest Caucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Caucasian_languages), most notably including [Adyghe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adyghe_language)). [Maltese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maltese_language) is the only [Semitic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_language) that is official within the EU, while [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_language) is the only European [language isolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_isolate).

Multilingualism and the protection of regional and minority languages are recognised political goals in Europe today. The [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) [Framework Convention for the Protection of National Minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framework_Convention_for_the_Protection_of_National_Minorities) and the Council of Europe's [European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Charter_for_Regional_or_Minority_Languages) set up a legal framework for language rights in Europe.

[Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_language) is spoken throughout Europe by the Roma minority.[^cite_note-306]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Europe)

Religion in Europe according to the *Global Religious Landscape* survey by the [Pew Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), 2016

[^cite_note-Survey-7]

1. [Christianity](5211.md) (76.2%)

2. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) (18.3%)

3. [Islam](6037917.md) (4.90%)

4. [Buddhism](3267529.md) (0.20%)

5. [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) (0.20%)

6. Folk religion (0.10%)

7. Other religions (0.10%)

The largest religion in Europe is [Christianity](5211.md), with 76.2% of Europeans considering themselves [Christians](18337522.md),[^cite_note-Christianity-307][^cite_note-308] including [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic), [Eastern Orthodox](10186.md) and various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) denominations. Among Protestants, the most popular are [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism) and the [Reformed faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_faith). Smaller Protestant denominations include [Anabaptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anabaptists) as well as denominations centred in the United States such as [Pentecostalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism), [Methodism](20119.md), and [Evangelicalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestants). Although Christianity originated in the Middle East, its centre of mass shifted to Europe when it [became the official religion of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_as_the_Roman_state_religion) in the late 4th century. Christianity played [a prominent role in the development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_Christianity_in_civilization) of the [European culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_culture) and [identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_identity).[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] Today, just over 25% of the world's Christians live in Europe.[^cite_note-312]

[Islam](6037917.md) is the second most popular religion in Europe. Over 25 million, or roughly 5% of the population, adhere to it.[^cite_note-pewresearch.org-313] In [Albania](738.md) and [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md), two countries in the [Balkan peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_peninsula) in Southeastern Europe, Islam instead of Christianity is the majority religion. This is also the case in [Turkey](11125639.md) and in [certain parts of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Russia), as well as in [Azerbaijan](746.md) and [Kazakhstan](16642.md), all of which are at the border to Asia.[^cite_note-pewresearch.org-313] Many countries in Europe are home to a sizeable Muslim minority, and [immigration to Europe](15497819.md) has increased the number of Muslim people in Europe in recent years.

The [Jewish](15624.md) population in Europe was about 1.4 million people in 2020 (about 0.2% of the population).[^cite_note-auto-314] There is a long [history of Jewish life in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Europe), beginning in antiquity. During the late 19th and early 20th centuries, the Russian Empire had the majority of the world's Jews living within its borders.[^cite_note-315] In 1897, according to [Russian census of 1897](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire_Census), the total Jewish population of Russia was 5.1 million people, which was 4.13% of total population. Of this total, the vast majority lived within the [Pale of Settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pale_of_Settlement).[^cite_note-316] In 1933, there were about 9.5 million Jewish people in Europe, representing 1.7% of the population,[^cite_note-317] but most were killed, and most of the rest displaced, during [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-auto-314] In the 21st century, [France](5843419.md) has the largest [Jewish population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population) in Europe, followed by the United Kingdom, [Germany](11867.md) and Russia.[^cite_note-Survey-7]

Other religions practiced in Europe include [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) and [Buddhism](3267529.md), which are minority religions, except in Russia's [Republic of Kalmykia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Kalmykia), where Tibetan Buddhism is the majority religion.

A large and increasing number of people in Europe are [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion), [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) and [agnostic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism). They are estimated to make up about 18.3% of Europe's population currently.[^cite_note-Survey-7]

### Major cities and urban areas

Further information: [List of European cities by population within city limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_cities_by_population_within_city_limits)

The three largest [urban areas of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_areas_in_Europe) are [Moscow](19004.md), [London](17867.md) and [Paris](22989.md). All have over 10 million residents,[^cite_note-UN_WUP_2016-319] and as such have been described as [megacities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megacity).[^cite_note-320] While [Istanbul](3391396.md) has the highest total city population, it lies partly in [Asia](689.md). 64.9% of the residents live on the European side and 35.1% on the Asian side. The next largest cities in order of population are [Madrid](41188263.md), [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md), [Milan](36511.md), [Barcelona](4443.md), [Berlin](3354.md), and [Rome](25458.md) each having over three million residents.[^cite_note-UN_WUP_2016-319]

When considering the commuter belts or [metropolitan areas within Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe) (for which comparable data is available), Moscow covers the largest population, followed in order by Istanbul, London, Paris, Madrid, Milan, Ruhr Area, Saint Petersburg, Rhein-Süd, Barcelona and Berlin.[^cite_note-321]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe)

Further information: [European folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_folklore) and [European art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_art)



Map purportedly displaying the European continent split along cultural and state borders as proposed by the German organisation [Ständiger Ausschuss für geographische Namen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Committee_on_Geographical_Names_\(Germany\)) (StAGN)

"Europe" as a cultural concept is substantially derived from the shared heritage of [ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) and its cultures. The boundaries of Europe were historically understood as those of [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) (or more specifically [Latin Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Christendom)), as established or defended throughout the medieval and early modern history of Europe, especially [against Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests), as in the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista) and the [Ottoman wars in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_wars_in_Europe).[^cite_note-322]

This shared cultural heritage is combined by overlapping indigenous national cultures and folklores, roughly divided into [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Europe), [Latin (Romance)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance-speaking_Europe) and Germanic, but with several components not part of either of these groups (notably [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_culture), [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_culture) and [Celtic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_Europe)). Historically, special examples with overlapping cultures are [Strasbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strasbourg) with Latin (Romance) and Germanic, or [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste) with Latin, Slavic and Germanic roots. Cultural contacts and mixtures shape a large part of the regional cultures of Europe. Europe is often described as "maximum cultural diversity with minimal geographical distances".

Different cultural events are organised in Europe, with the aim of bringing different cultures closer together and raising awareness of their importance, such as the [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture), the [European Region of Gastronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Region_of_Gastronomy), the [European Youth Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Youth_Capital) and the European Capital of Sport.

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Europe)

Sport in Europe tends to be highly organised with many sports having professional leagues. The origins of many of the world's most popular sports today lie in the codification of many traditional games, especially in the United Kingdom. However, a paradoxical feature of European sport is the extent to which local, regional and national variations continue to exist, and even in some instances to predominate.[^cite_note-323]

### Social dimension

In Europe, many people are unable to access basic social conditions, which makes it harder for them to thrive and flourish. Access to basic necessities can be compromised, for example 10% of Europeans spend at least 40% of household income on housing. 75 million Europeans feel [socially isolated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_isolation). From the 1980s income inequality has been rising and wage shares have been falling. In 2016, the richest 20% of households earned over five times more than the poorest 20%. Many workers experience stagnant [real wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wages) and [precarious work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precarious_work) is common even for [essential workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_workers).[^cite_note-SustainableProsperity-324]
